I'm not confused, just forgetful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble learning new things since you started noticing the forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any trouble with that. I mean, I'm not exactly taking classes at my age or anything, but if I need to figure out how to use a new tool or fix something different around the house, I can still pick it up just fine. It really is just the remembering part that gives me grief, not the learning.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself when I realize I've forgotten my tools or missed an appointment again, but I think that's pretty normal. Other than that, my overall mood hasn't really changed. I'm not feeling down or angry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can hold a conversation just fine and I don't get stuck looking for words. It's just the memory for tasks and items that's slipping, not my speech.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I'm pretty much the same guy I've always been. I mean, like I said earlier, I get a little annoyed with myself when I mess up and forget something, but my actual personality hasn't changed at all. Nobody's told me I'm acting differently, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other significant medical issues or changes in your physical health since the cardiac scare?\n\nPatient: No, my health has been pretty steady since then. I haven't had any other major medical problems or anything like that. Just the usual aches and pains of getting older, but nothing new or significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble solving problems or making decisions since you started noticing the forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any trouble with that. I can still figure things out and make decisions just fine. It's really just the memory for where I put things or what I was supposed to do that's bothering me. But as far as my actual thinking or, you know, deciding what to do... no, that's all still working fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, uh, my heart follow-up appointments, or remembering to call the clinic back. Even just regular stuff—I’ll go to do a repair job around the house and realize I showed up without the right tools. It's just happening a lot, and I figured I should finally come in and talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice you were forgetting things more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been causing real trouble for... I'd say about a year and two months now. It really started getting bad right after I had a cardiac scare. Because of that, I was forced to cut way back on my walking, and, uh, the projects around the house that used to keep me settled. Once I couldn't do all that as much, I just started noticing the forgetfulness a lot more.\n\nDoctor: Has your forgetfulness been interfering with your daily routine or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. It’s gotten to the point where the senior-center repair group just can’t count on me the way they used to. And... well, my doctor’s office has actually had to chase me down about those missed heart follow-ups I mentioned. So, yeah, it's definitely getting in the way of my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel confused or disoriented?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't get lost or forget where I am, if that's what you mean. It's really just... forgetting to do things, or showing up without my tools. But I always know what's going on around me. I'm not confused, just forgetful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble learning new things since you started noticing the forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any trouble with that. I mean, I'm not exactly taking classes at my age or anything, but if I need to figure out how to use a new tool or fix something different around the house, I can still pick it up just fine. It really is just the remembering part that gives me grief, not the learning.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself when I realize I've forgotten my tools or missed an appointment again, but I think that's pretty normal. Other than that, my overall mood hasn't really changed. I'm not feeling down or angry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can hold a conversation just fine and I don't get stuck looking for words. It's just the memory for tasks and items that's slipping, not my speech.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I'm pretty much the same guy I've always been. I mean, like I said earlier, I get a little annoyed with myself when I mess up and forget something, but my actual personality hasn't changed at all. Nobody's told me I'm acting differently, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other significant medical issues or changes in your physical health since the cardiac scare?\n\nPatient: No, my health has been pretty steady since then. I haven't had any other major medical problems or anything like that. Just the usual aches and pains of getting older, but nothing new or significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble solving problems or making decisions since you started noticing the forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any trouble with that. I can still figure things out and make decisions just fine. It's really just the memory for where I put things or what I was supposed to do that's bothering me. But as far as my actual thinking or, you know, deciding what to do... no, that's all still working fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention or focusing on things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do have some trouble with that. Like, I'll sit down at the bridge table, or I go to these planning meetings for this group I'm in, the Fix-It Friends. And I really mean to pay close attention, I do. But after a little while, my mind just kind of... slides off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing your daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have much trouble planning my day out. I mean, I'm retired, so it's not like I have a strict work schedule to juggle anymore, but... I know what I need to get done and I organize it just fine. I manage all my errands and activities without any real issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your cardiac scare?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. My memory from back then is clear as a bell. I can remember all the years before that heart scare without any trouble. I'd say about a year and two months now. It really started getting bad right after I had a cardiac scare. Like, I'll sit down at the bridge table, or I go to these planning meetings for this group I'm in, the Fix-It Friends. And I really mean to pay close attention, I do. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing your daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I manage all my errands and activities without any real issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your cardiac scare?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I'd say about a year and two months now. It really started getting bad right after I had a cardiac scare. It’s gotten to the point where the senior-center repair group just can’t count on me the way they used to. And... well, my doctor’s office has actually had to chase me down about those missed heart follow-ups I mentioned. So, yeah, it's definitely getting in the way of my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel confused or disoriented?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't get lost or forget where I am, if that's what you mean. It's really just... forgetting to do things, or showing up without my tools. But I always know what's going on around me. I'm not confused, just forgetful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble learning new things since you started noticing the forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any trouble with that. I mean, I'm not exactly taking classes at my age or anything, but if I need to figure out how to use a new tool or fix something different around the house, I can still pick it up just fine. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I just keep forgetting things I really shouldn’t. Important things, you know? Like, uh, my heart follow-up appointments, or remembering to call the clinic back. Even just regular stuff—I’ll go to do a repair job around the house and realize I showed up without the right tools. It's just happening a lot, and I figured I should finally come in and talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice you were forgetting things more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been causing real trouble for... I'd say about a year and two months now. It really started getting bad right after I had a cardiac scare. Because of that, I was forced to cut way back on my walking, and, uh, the projects around the house that used to keep me settled. Once I couldn't do all that as much, I just started noticing the forgetfulness a lot more.\n\nDoctor: Has your forgetfulness been interfering with your daily routine or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. It’s gotten to the point where the senior-center repair group just can’t count on me the way they used to. And... well, my doctor’s office has actually had to chase me down about those missed heart follow-ups I mentioned. So, yeah, it's definitely getting in the way of my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel confused or disoriented?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't get lost or forget where I am, if that's what you mean. It's really just... forgetting to do things, or showing up without my tools. But I always know what's going on around me. I'm not confused, just forgetful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble learning new things since you started noticing the forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any trouble with that. I mean, I'm not exactly taking classes at my age or anything, but if I need to figure out how to use a new tool or fix something different around the house, I can still pick it up just fine. It really is just the remembering part that gives me grief, not the learning.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself when I realize I've forgotten my tools or missed an appointment again, but I think that's pretty normal. Other than that, my overall mood hasn't really changed. I'm not feeling down or angry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can hold a conversation just fine and I don't get stuck looking for words. It's just the memory for tasks and items that's slipping, not my speech.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I'm pretty much the same guy I've always been. I mean, like I said earlier, I get a little annoyed with myself when I mess up and forget something, but my actual personality hasn't changed at all. Nobody's told me I'm acting differently, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other significant medical issues or changes in your physical health since the cardiac scare?\n\nPatient: No, my health has been pretty steady since then. I haven't had any other major medical problems or anything like that. Just the usual aches and pains of getting older, but nothing new or significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble solving problems or making decisions since you started noticing the forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any trouble with that. I can still figure things out and make decisions just fine. It's really just the memory for where I put things or what I was supposed to do that's bothering me. But as far as my actual thinking or, you know, deciding what to do... no, that's all still working fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention or focusing on things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do have some trouble with that. Like, I'll sit down at the bridge table, or I go to these planning meetings for this group I'm in, the Fix-It Friends. And I really mean to pay close attention, I do. But after a little while, my mind just kind of... slides off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing your daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have much trouble planning my day out. I mean, I'm retired, so it's not like I have a strict work schedule to juggle anymore, but... I know what I need to get done and I organize it just fine. I manage all my errands and activities without any real issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your cardiac scare?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. My memory from back then is clear as a bell. I can remember all the years before that heart scare without any trouble. It's really just the day-to-day stuff lately that trips me up, but the past... no, that's all still right there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble performing tasks that you've done many times before, like cooking or taking care of personal business?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any problems with that. I can still cook my meals and handle my bills and all that just fine. It's just the same as it's always been. I don't get confused about how to do things I've done a million times before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness might be related to your reduced physical activity after the cardiac scare?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it probably is. This has all been causing real trouble for... well, about a year and two months now. Ever since that cardiac scare forced me to cut back on all the walking and the projects I used to do. Those things really used to keep me settled, and without them... yeah, it's been a lot harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a lack of interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I still have plenty of interest in the things I like doing. Like those projects and the walks I mentioned earlier... I still want to do them just as much as I ever did. It's just that the heart scare forced me to step back from them a bit. But the actual desire to do them? No, that hasn't gone away at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble understanding or following visual information, like maps or diagrams?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any issues with that. I can still read a map or figure out a diagram just fine. That hasn't been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the time since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been feeling sad or depressed. I mean, I get frustrated sometimes that I can't do as much as I used to, but it's not sadness. I'm just antsy and forgetful, not down in the dumps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your doctor has attributed your forgetfulness to your cardiac scare or any other medical condition?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. My regular doctor hasn't... well, they haven't said it's because of the heart scare or any other specific medical issue. We haven't really figured out a reason for it yet. That's kind of why I'm here talking to you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started experiencing forgetfulness?\n\nPatient: No, my sleep hasn't really changed. I mean, I sleep about the same as I always have. I haven't noticed any difference there since the forgetfulness started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
